These examples indicate that the configuration of the local relief is decisive in identifying regional types of valleys. Likewise, due to their warm climate, the Guayana valleys are distinguished from the temperate or cold Andean valleys by their humid environment. Both are, in turn, different from the semi-arid depressions of the states of Lara and Falcón.

The Andean valleys, essentially agricultural, precociously populated but nowadays in loss of speed, do not confront the same problems of space occupation as the strongly urbanized and industrialized valleys of the central section of the Cordillera de la Costa. On the other hand, the unpopulated and practically untouched [Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guiana) valleys are another category this area is called the Lost World (*Mundo Perdido*).[^cite_note-\:1-210]

The Andean valleys are undoubtedly the most impressive of the Venezuelan territory because of the energy of the encasing reliefs, whose summits often dominate the valley bottoms by 3,000 to 3,500 meters of relative altitude. They are also the most picturesque in terms of their style of habitat, forms of land use, handicraft production and all the traditions linked to these activities.[^cite_note-\:1-210]

### Deserts



[Médanos de Coro National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9danos_de_Coro_National_Park), [Paraguaná Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraguan%C3%A1_Peninsula), [Falcón State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falc%C3%B3n_State), Venezuela

Venezuela has a great diversity of landscapes and climates,[^cite_note-212] including arid and dry areas. The main desert in the country is in the state of Falcon near the city of Coro. It is protected as the [Médanos de Coro National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9danos_de_Coro_National_Park).[^cite_note-213] The park is the largest of its kind in Venezuela, covering 91 square kilometres. The landscape is dotted with cacti and other xerophytic plants that can survive in humidity-free conditions near the desert. Other desert areas in the country include part of the Guajira Desert in the Guajira Municipality in the north of Zulia State[^cite_note-214] and facing the [Gulf of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Venezuela), the Médanos de Capanaparo[^cite_note-215] in the Santos Luzardo National Park in [Apure State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apure_State), the Medanos de la Isla de Zapara[^cite_note-216] in Zulia State, the so-called Hundición de Yay[^cite_note-217] in the Andrés Eloy Blanco Municipality of Lara State, and the Urumaco Formation also in Falcón State.

Desert wildlife includes mostly lizards, iguanas and other reptiles. Although less frequent, the desert is home to some foxes, giant anteaters and rabbits. There are also some native bird populations, such as the sparrowhawk, tropical mockingbird, scaly dove and crested quail.

### Earthquakes

Main article: [List of earthquakes in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_earthquakes_in_Venezuela)

[Map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:Map/6/10.5/-66/en)

Map of earthquakes in Venezuela since 1900, with magnitudes. Click for dates and details. ([map data](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Data\:Earthquakes%20in%20Venezuela%201900-.map))

Venezuela is located in a seismically active region, making it susceptible to earthquakes.[^cite_note-s236-218] Historically, [Venezuela has experienced numerous earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_earthquakes_in_Venezuela), some of which have caused massive damage. The most notable are the [1812 Caracas earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1812_Caracas_earthquake), a magnitude of 7.4 caused extensive damage in [Caracas](55289.md), [La Guaira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Guaira), [Barquisimeto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barquisimeto), [San Felipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Felipe,_Yaracuy), and [Mérida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9rida,_M%C3%A9rida).[^cite_note-i006-219] The [1967 Caracas earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Caracas_earthquake), with its epicenter being 20 km (12 mi) from the Venezuelan capital Caracas that registered a magnitude of 6.3.[^cite_note-k810-220] The [1997 Cariaco earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Cariaco_earthquake), a magnitude 6.9 thats epicenter was in the [Cariaco Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cariaco_Basin).[^cite_note-a090-221] The [2018 earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_2018_Venezuela_earthquake), at a magnitude 7.3 that occurred near [Sucre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sucre_\(state\)),[^cite_note-t410-222] and the [2026 Venezuela earthquakes](83547391.md), a [strike-slip earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike-slip_earthquakes) affected northwestern and central Venezuela.[^cite_note-b557-223]

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Government of Venezuela](78643.md), [Cabinet of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Venezuela), [Politics of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Venezuela), [Elections in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Venezuela), and [Democracy in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_Venezuela)



[Delcy Rodríguez](44199228.md)\
[Acting President](3329692.md)



*Vacant*\
[Vice President](23535071.md)



The [National Assembly of Venezuela](510768.md) [building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_Federal_Legislativo)

Two major blocs of [political parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Venezuela) are in Venezuela; the incumbent leftist bloc [United Socialist Party of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Socialist_Party_of_Venezuela) (PSUV), its major allies [Fatherland for All](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatherland_for_All) (PPT) and the [Communist Party of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Venezuela) (PCV), and the opposition bloc grouped into the electoral coalition [Mesa de la Unidad Democrática](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesa_de_la_Unidad_Democr%C3%A1tica). This includes [A New Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_New_Era) (UNT) together with allied parties [Project Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Venezuela), [Justice First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_First), [Movement for Socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_for_Socialism_\(Venezuela\)) (MAS) and others.

The Venezuelan president is elected by a vote, with direct and [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage), and is both [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state) and [head of government](37570.md). The term of office is six years. As of February 2009, a president may be re-elected an unlimited number of times. The president appoints the vice president, decides the size and composition of the cabinet, and makes appointments to it with the involvement of the legislature. The president can ask the legislature to reconsider portions of laws they find objectionable, but a simple parliamentary majority can override these objections. The president may ask the National Assembly to pass an [enabling act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enabling_act#In_Venezuela) granting the ability to [rule by decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_by_decree) in specified policy areas. This requires a two-thirds majority in the Assembly. Since 1959, six Venezuelan presidents have been granted such powers.

The [unicameral](204304.md) Venezuelan parliament is the *Asamblea Nacional* ("National Assembly"). The number of members is variable – each state and the Capital district elect three representatives, plus the result of dividing the state's population by 1.1% of the country's population.[^cite_note-224] Three seats are reserved for representatives of Venezuela's Indigenous peoples. For the 2011–2016 period, the number of seats is 165.[^cite_note-225] All deputies serve five-year terms.

The [legal system of Venezuela](28286365.md) belongs to the [continental law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_law) tradition. The highest judicial body is the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)) or *Tribunal Supremo de Justicia*, whose magistrates are elected by parliament for a single twelve-year term. The [National Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_\(Venezuela\)) (*Consejo Nacional Electoral*, or *CNE*) is in charge of electoral processes. It is formed by five main directors elected by the National Assembly. Supreme Court president Luisa Estela Morales said in December 2009 that Venezuela had moved away from "a rigid division of powers" toward a system characterized by "intense coordination" between the branches of government. Morales clarified that each power must be independent.[^cite_note-226]

In 2006, the [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md) rated Venezuela an [hybrid regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_regime) and the third least [democratic government in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_the_Americas) on the [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Index).[^cite_note-227] The Democracy Index downgraded Venezuela to an [authoritarian regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_regime) in 2017, citing continued increasingly dictatorial behaviors by the Maduro government.[^cite_note-228] The [Regimes of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regimes_of_the_World) index rated Venezuela as an [electoral autocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_autocracy) in 2024.[^cite_note-j496-229]

Since the [2026 United States intervention in Venezuela](82021123.md), it has been alleged that the [United States](3434750.md) effectively controls Venezuela's government as a [puppet state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puppet_state) through the [US Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Secretary_of_State) (SecState), who has been called the "[viceroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroy)" by some sources. Venezuela's revenues are routed through the [US Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Treasury), where they are then given back to Venezuela with conditions on how they can be spent. Government appointments, such as the minister of defense, are run through approval from the US Secretary of State. The [Executive Office of the President of the United States](44029.md) has controlled the public appearances and statements by the country's leader, including alleged *de facto* control over its foreign policy. Through control over oil sanctions, American oil companies have been provided access to Venezuela's oil reserves and pressured Venezuelan state-owned oil company [PDVSA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PDVSA) to take over assets owned by rival nations and competitors.[^cite_note-NYT_07112026-10][^cite_note-Callaghan_01042026-11]

### Suspension of constitutional rights

See also: [Censorship in Venezuela](19533311.md)



Protests in [Altamira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altamira_\(Caracas\)), Caracas, 2014

The [2015 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election) elected the 164 deputies and three Indigenous representatives of the National Assembly. In 2014, protests began amid hyperinflation, civil unrest, and shortages. The protests were largely peaceful.[^cite_note-SVCOprotestINFO-230] The government accused the protest of being motivated by fascists, opposition leaders, capitalism and foreign influence,[^cite_note-Milne-231] President Maduro acknowledged PSUV defeat but attributed the opposition's victory to an intensification of an economic war. Despite this, Maduro said, "I will stop by hook or by crook the opposition coming to power, whatever the costs, in any way".[^cite_note-232]

In the following months, Maduro fulfilled his promise of preventing the elected National Assembly from legislating. The first steps taken by PSUV and the government were the substitution of the entire Supreme Court a day after the parliamentary elections,[^cite_note-prodavinci.com-233] contrary to the Constitution of Venezuela, acclaimed as fraudulent by the majority of the Venezuelan and international press.[^cite_note-larazon.net-234][^cite_note-el-nacional.com-235][^cite_note-bbc.com-236][^cite_note-ntn24web.com-237] The *Financial Times* described the function of the Supreme Court in Venezuela as "rubber stamping executive whims and vetoing legislation".[^cite_note-broken_system-238] The PSUV government used this violation to suspend several elected opponents.[^cite_note-239] Maduro said that "the Amnesty law (approved by the Parliament) will not be executed" and asked the Supreme Court to declare it unconstitutional.[^cite_note-240]

In January 2016, Maduro approved an unconstitutional economic emergency decree,[^cite_note-241] relegating to his own figure the legislative and executive powers, while also holding judiciary power through the fraudulent designation of judges the day after the election on 6 December 2015.[^cite_note-prodavinci.com-233][^cite_note-larazon.net-234][^cite_note-el-nacional.com-235][^cite_note-ntn24web.com-237] From these events, Maduro effectively controls all three branches of government. In May 2016, constitutional guarantees were suspended when Maduro decreed the extension of the economic emergency decree for another 60 days and declared a state of emergency,[^cite_note-242] which was a violation of the constitution[^cite_note-243] As a result, the [Organization of American States](22788.md) considered the application of the [Inter-American Democratic Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-American_Democratic_Charter)[^cite_note-244] sanctions for non-compliance to its own constitution. In March 2017, the Venezuelan Supreme Court took over law-making powers from the National Assembly[^cite_note-245] but reversed its decision the following day.[^cite_note-246]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [States of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_of_Venezuela) and [Regions of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Venezuela)



A map of the Venezuelan federation

Venezuela is divided into 23 states (*estados*), a capital district (*distrito capital*) for Caracas, and the Federal Dependencies (*Dependencias Federales*, a special territory). States are subdivided into 335 municipalities (*municipios*). These are subdivided into parishes (*parroquias*). The states are grouped into nine administrative regions (*regiones administrativas*), which were established in 1969 by presidential decree.

Venezuela maintains a claim on the territory it calls "Guayana Esequiba", the territory administered by Guyana west of the Esequibo River. In 1966, the British and Venezuelan governments signed the Geneva Agreement to resolve the conflict peacefully.[^cite_note-247] The [Port of Spain Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Spain_Protocol) of 1970 set a deadline to try to resolve the issue, without success to date.

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Venezuela](32461.md)



The [disputed "Guayana Esequiba" claim area](6896458.md) is a territory administered by [Guyana](17238691.md) and historically claimed by Venezuela.

Throughout most of the 20th century, Venezuela maintained friendly relations with most Latin American and Western nations. Relations with the United States government worsened in 2002 after the coup attempt during which the U.S. government recognized the short-lived interim presidency of [Pedro Carmona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Carmona). In 2015, Venezuela was declared a national security threat by U.S. President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama).[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] As a result, Venezuela strengthened ties with the anti-west governments of [Turkey](11125639.md), [Russia](25391.md), [Iran](14653.md), [China](5405.md), [DPRK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DPRK), [Myanmar](19457.md) and [Cuba](5042481.md).[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252]

Venezuela seeks an alternative [hemispheric](73162.md) integration through initiatives such as the [Bolivarian Alternative for the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Alternative_for_the_Americas) trade proposal and the state-sponsored Latin American television network [teleSUR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TeleSUR). Venezuela is one of five nations in the world—along with Russia, Nicaragua, Nauru, and Syria—to have recognized the independence of [Abkhazia and South Ossetia](19042397.md). Venezuela was a proponent of [OAS](22788.md)'s decision to adopt its Anti-Corruption Convention[^cite_note-253] and is actively working in the [Mercosur](25869317.md) trade bloc to push increased trade and energy integration. Globally, it seeks a "[multi-polar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polarity_in_international_relations)" world based on strengthened ties among undeveloped countries.



President Maduro among other Latin American leaders participating in a 2017 [ALBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ALBA) gathering

Venezuela is a charter member of the [United Nations](31769.md) (UN), [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS), [Union of South American Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_South_American_Nations) (UNASUR), [Bolivarian Alliance for the Peoples of Our America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Alliance_for_the_Peoples_of_Our_America) (ALBA), [Mercosur](25869317.md), [Latin American Integration Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_Integration_Association) (LAIA) and [Organization of Ibero-American States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Ibero-American_States) (OEI).

In 2017 Venezuela announced its intention to withdraw from the OAS.[^cite_note-254] It would take two years for the country to leave formally. During this period, the country was not to plan on participating in the OAS.[^cite_note-255] Venezuela may suffer a deterioration of its power in international affairs if the global [transition to renewable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transition_to_renewable_energy) is completed. It is ranked 151 out of 156 countries in the index of Geopolitical Gains and Losses after energy transition.[^cite_note-256]

### Military

Main article: [Bolivarian National Armed Forces of Venezuela](32460.md)

### Law and crime

Main articles: [Law of Venezuela](28286365.md) and [Crime in Venezuela](37476256.md)



The murder rate per 100,000 citizens, 1998 to 2018. \
Sources: OVV,

[^cite_note-257]

[^cite_note-FUSIONmr-258]

[^cite_note-UNODC2011-259]

In Venezuela, a person is murdered every 21 minutes.[^cite_note-265] Violent crimes have been so prevalent that the government no longer produces the crime data.[^cite_note-266] In 2013, the homicide rate was approximately 79 per 100,000, one of the world's highest, having quadrupled in the past 15 years with over 200,000 people murdered.[^cite_note-267] By 2015, it had risen to 90 per 100,000.[^cite_note-BBCVenezuela-268] Caracas has one of the greatest homicide rates of any large city in the world, with 122 homicides per 100,000 residents.[^cite_note-269]

In 2008, polls indicated that crime was the number one concern of voters.[^cite_note-270] Attempts at fighting crime such as Operation Liberation of the People were implemented to crack down on gang-controlled areas,[^cite_note-271] but of reported criminal acts less than 2% are prosecuted.[^cite_note-Finnegan-272] In 2017, the *Financial Times* noted that some of the arms procured by the government over the previous two decades had been diverted to paramilitary civilian groups and criminal syndicates.[^cite_note-broken_system-238]

Venezuela is especially dangerous for foreign travelers and investors who are visiting. The [United States Department of State](31975.md) and the [government of Canada](616255.md) have warned foreign visitors that they may be subjected to robbery, kidnapping[^cite_note-273] and murder, and that their own diplomatic travelers are required to travel in armored vehicles.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] The United Kingdom's [Foreign and Commonwealth Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Office) has advised against all travel to Venezuela.[^cite_note-276] Visitors have been murdered during robberies.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278]

There are approximately 33 prisons holding about 50,000 inmates.[^cite_note-279] The prison system is heavily overcrowded; its facilities have capacity for only 14,000 prisoners.[^cite_note-280]

### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Venezuela](23438021.md)

Human rights organizations such as [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) and [Amnesty International](18947898.md) have increasingly criticized Venezuela's human rights record, with the former organization noting in 2017 that the Chavez and subsequently the Maduro government have increasingly concentrated power in the executive branch, eroded constitutional human rights protections and allowed the government to persecute and repress its critics and opposition.[^cite_note-281] Other persistent concerns as noted by the report included poor prison conditions, the continuous [harassment of independent media](37784.md) and human rights defenders by the government.

### Corruption

Main article: [Corruption in Venezuela](23453241.md)



The [United States Department of Justice](52563.md)'s 2020 graphic titled *Corrupt Venezuelan Regime*

Corruption in Venezuela is high by global standards and was so for much of the 20th century. The discovery of oil worsened political corruption.[^cite_note-282] By the late 1970s, [Juan Pablo Pérez Alfonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Pablo_P%C3%A9rez_Alfonso)'s description of oil as "the Devil's excrement" had become a common expression in Venezuela.[^cite_note-283] The [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) has ranked Venezuela as one of the most corrupt countries since the survey started in 1995. The 2010 ranking placed Venezuela at 164 out of 178 ranked countries in government transparency.[^cite_note-284] By 2016, the rank had increased to 166 out of 178.[^cite_note-285] The [World Justice Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Justice_Project) ranked Venezuela 99th out of 99 countries surveyed in its 2014 Rule of Law Index.[^cite_note-286]

This corruption is shown with [Venezuela's significant involvement](28018284.md) in drug trafficking, with Colombian cocaine and other drugs transiting Venezuela towards the United States and Europe. In the period 2003–2008, authorities seized the fifth-largest total quantity of cocaine in the world.[^cite_note-287] In 2006, the *[Oficina Nacional Antidrogas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oficina_Nacional_Antidrogas)* was incorporated into the office of the vice-president. However, many major government and military officials have been known for their involvement with drug trafficking.[^cite_note-288]

## Economy

|  | This section should include  only a brief summary of {{{1}}}. See [Wikipedia\:Summary style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Summary_style) for information on how to properly incorporate it into this article's main text. |
| --- | --- |

Main article: [Economy of Venezuela](32457.md)

Annual variation of real GDP according to the Central Bank of Venezuela (2016 preliminary)[^cite_note-BCV2016-289][^cite_note-290] View [source data](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Data\:Venezuela%20annual%20variation%20of%20real%20GDP.tab).



Millennium Mall, one of the main [shopping centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shopping_centers) in [Caracas](55289.md)

> Almost 82 per cent of Venezuelans live in poverty, with 53 per cent in extreme poverty, unable to buy even basic foodstuffs.
>
> – A UN special rapporteur said in February 2024 after visiting the country.[^cite_note-u571-291]

Venezuela has a market-based [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) dominated by the petroleum sector,[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] which accounts for roughly a third of GDP, around 80% of exports, and more than half of government revenues. Per capita GDP for 2016 was estimated at US\$15,100, ranking 109th in the world.[^cite_note-ciawfb-70] Historically, Venezuela had the [least expensive petrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gasoline_usage_and_pricing) in the world because the consumer price of petrol was heavily subsidized. The private sector controls two-thirds of Venezuela's economy.[^cite_note-294] Venezuela was ranked 136th out of 139 ranks in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] At least 5% of Venezuela's economy, as of 2022, relied on remittances.[^cite_note-297]

The [Central Bank of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_Venezuela) is responsible for developing [monetary policy](297032.md) for the [Venezuelan bolívar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_bol%C3%ADvar) which is used as currency. The president of the Central Bank of Venezuela serves as the country's representative in the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md). The U.S.-based conservative think tank [the Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation) claims Venezuela has the weakest property rights in the world, scoring only 5.0 on a scale of 100. [Expropriation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expropriation) without compensation is not uncommon.

As of 2011, more than 60% of Venezuela's international reserves was in gold, eight times more than the average for the region. Most of its gold held abroad was located in London. In November 2011, the first of US\$11 billion of repatriated gold bullion arrived in Caracas. Chávez called the repatriation of gold a "sovereign" step that would help protect the country's foreign reserves from the turmoil in the U.S. and Europe.[^cite_note-298] Government policies quickly spent down this returned gold, and in 2013 the government was forced to add the dollar reserves of state-owned companies to those of the national bank to reassure the international bond market.[^cite_note-299]

Manufacturing contributed 17% of GDP in 2006. Venezuela manufactures and exports heavy industry products such as steel, aluminium and cement, with production concentrated around Ciudad Guayana, near the [Guri Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guri_Dam), one of the largest in the world and the provider of about three-quarters of Venezuela's electricity. Other notable manufacturing includes electronics and automobiles, as well as beverages, and foodstuffs. [Agriculture in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Venezuela) accounts for approximately 3% of GDP, 10% of the labor force, and at least a quarter of land area.

Since the discovery of oil in the early 20th century, Venezuela has been one of the world's leading exporters of oil, and it is a founding member of the [OPEC](166346.md) cartel.[^cite_note-g417-300] Previously an underdeveloped exporter of agricultural commodities, oil quickly came to dominate exports and government revenues. The 1980s oil glut led to an external debt crisis and a long-running economic crisis, which saw inflation peak at 100% in 1996.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKellyPalma2006207-301] The 1990s also saw Venezuela experience a [major banking crisis in 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_banking_crisis_of_1994).

The recovery of oil prices after 2001 boosted the economy and facilitated social spending. With social programs such as the Bolivarian Missions, Venezuela initially made progress in social development in the 2000s, particularly in areas such as health, education, and poverty. Many of the social policies pursued by the Chávez administration were jump-started by the [Millennium Development Goals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennium_Development_Goals), eight goals that Venezuela and 188 other nations agreed to in September 2000.[^cite_note-302] The sustainability of the Bolivarian Missions has been questioned due to the Bolivarian state's overspending on public works and because the Chávez government did not save funds for future economic hardships, with economic issues and poverty rising as a result of their policies in the 2010s.[^cite_note-ELPAISfeb2015-303][^cite_note-CSM25march-304][^cite_note-FPdontblame-305]

In 2003, the government implemented currency controls after [capital flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_flight) led to a devaluation of the currency. This led to the development of a parallel market of dollars in the subsequent years. The [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) led to a renewed economic downturn. Despite controversial data shared by the Venezuelan government showing that the country had halved malnutrition following one of the UN's Millennium Development Goals,[^cite_note-ECONeatCHAVISMO-306][^cite_note-307] shortages of staple goods began to occur in Venezuela and malnutrition began to increase.[^cite_note-ECONeatCHAVISMO-306] In early 2013, Venezuela devalued its currency due to shortages,[^cite_note-WSJ-Devalue-Currency-308][^cite_note-\:8-309] including necessities such as toilet paper, milk, and flour.[^cite_note-TheGuardian-Food-Shortages-310] Fears rose so high due to the toilet paper shortage that the government occupied a toilet paper factory and continued plans to nationalize other industrial aspects like food distribution.[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312] Bond ratings have also decreased multiple times in 2013 due to decisions by the president Nicolás Maduro.[^cite_note-313] In 2016, consumer prices increased 800%, and the economy declined by 18.6%, entering an [economic depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_depression).[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315] The outlook was deemed negative by most bond-rating services in 2017.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317] For 2018 an inflation rate of 1,000,000 percent was projected, putting Venezuela in a similar situation to that in Germany in 1923 or Zimbabwe in the late 2000s.[^cite_note-318]

"The formerly rich petro-state has seen GDP fall by 80 percent in less than a decade, driving some seven million of its citizens to flee. Most Venezuelans live on just a few dollars a month, with the health care and education systems in total disrepair and biting shortages of electricity and fuel" as of 2024, according to [VOA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VOA) (report from [AFP](471981.md)).[^cite_note-h998-319]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Venezuela)



[Ángel Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angel_Falls) in [Canaima National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaima_National_Park) is the world's highest waterfall.



[Mochima National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mochima_National_Park), eastern Venezuela

Tourism has been developed considerably in recent decades, particularly because of its favorable geographical position, the variety of landscapes, the richness of plant and wildlife, the culture and the tropical climate. [Margarita Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margarita_Island) is one of the top tourist destinations. It is an island with a modern infrastructure, bordered by beaches suitable for extreme sports, and features castles, fortresses and churches of great cultural value. [Los Roques Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Roques_Archipelago) is made up of a set of islands and keys that constitute one of the main tourist attractions in the country. With exotic crystalline beaches, Morrocoy is a national park, formed by small keys very close to the mainland, which have grown rapidly as one of the greatest tourist attractions in the Venezuelan Caribbean.[^cite_note-320]

[Canaima National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaima_National_Park)[^cite_note-321] extends over 30,000 km<sup>2</sup> (12,000 sq mi) to the border with Guyana and Brazil; it is considered the sixth largest national park in the world. Its steep cliffs and waterfalls (including Angel Falls, which is the highest waterfall in the world, at 1,002 m) form spectacular landscapes. The state of Mérida[^cite_note-322] is one of the main tourist centers of Venezuela. Starting from [Mérida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9rida,_M%C3%A9rida) is the longest and highest cable car in the world, which reaches the Pico Espejo of 4,765 m.

### Shortages

Main article: [Shortages in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela)

Shortages in Venezuela have been prevalent following the enactment of price controls and other policies during the [economic policy of the Hugo Chávez government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez_government).[^cite_note-economist.com-323][^cite_note-qz.com-324] Under the [economic policy of the Nicolás Maduro government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_government), greater shortages occurred due to the policy of withholding United States dollars from importers with price controls.[^cite_note-WSJseptDOWNGRADE-325]



Empty shelves in a store in Venezuela due to shortages in 2014

Shortages occur in regulated products, such as milk, various types of meat, coffee, rice, oil, flour, butter, and other goods including basic necessities like toilet paper, personal hygiene products, and even medicine.[^cite_note-economist.com-323][^cite_note-ACN-326][^cite_note-El_Nuevo_Herald-327] As a result of the shortages, Venezuelans must search for food, wait in lines for hours and sometimes do without certain products.[^cite_note-\:7-328][^cite_note-REUTjan2015-329][^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332]

A drought, combined with a lack of planning and maintenance, caused a hydroelectricity shortage. To deal with lack of power supply, in April 2016 the Maduro government announced rolling blackouts[^cite_note-333] and reduced the government workweek to only Monday and Tuesday.[^cite_note-334] A multi-university study found that in 2016 alone, about 75% of Venezuelans lost weight due to hunger, with the average losing about 8.6 kg (19 lbs) due to the lack of food.[^cite_note-UPIfeb17-335] In March 2017, there were shortages of gasoline in some regions.[^cite_note-EImar17-336]

### Petroleum and other resources

See also: [History of the Venezuelan oil industry](16852238.md) and [Energy policy of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Venezuela)



Venezuela is estimated to have more proven crude oil reserves than any other country.

[^cite_note-SciAm_20260107-337]



Venezuela's exports of crude oil from January 2018 to December 2019

[^cite_note-EIA-VEN-338]

Venezuela's main petroleum deposits are located around and beneath Lake Maracaibo, the Gulf of Venezuela (both in [Zulia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulia)), and in the Orinoco Basin, where the largest reserve is located.[^cite_note-auto-339] Compared to 2009, another 40.4% in crude oil reserves were proven in 2010, allowing Venezuela to surpass Saudi Arabia as the country with the largest reserves of this type.[^cite_note-auto-339] Besides having the largest conventional oil reserves and the second-largest natural gas reserves in the Western Hemisphere,[^cite_note-bbc-340] Venezuela has non-conventional oil deposits, in the form of [extra-heavy crude oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_crude_oil), [bitumen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitumen) and [tar sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tar_sands), approximately equal to the world's reserves of conventional oil.[^cite_note-wec-341] The [electricity sector in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_Venezuela) is one of the few to rely primarily on [hydropower](14073.md), and includes the Guri Dam, one of the largest in the world.

In the first half of the 20th century, U.S. oil companies were heavily involved in Venezuela, initially interested only in purchasing concessions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYergin1991233–236,_432-342] In 1943, a new government introduced a 50/50 split in profits between the government and the oil industry. In 1960, with a newly installed democratic government, Hydrocarbons Minister Juan Pablo Pérez Alfonso led the creation of OPEC, the consortium of oil-producing countries aiming to support the price of oil.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYergin1991510–513-343]

In 1973, Venezuela voted to nationalize its oil industry outright, effective 1 January 1976, with Petróleos de Venezuela (PDVSA) taking over and presiding over a number of holding companies; in subsequent years, Venezuela built a vast refining and marketing system in the U.S. and Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYergin1991767-344] In the 1990s, PDVSA became more independent from the government and presided over an *apertura* (opening) in which it invited in foreign investment. Under Hugo Chávez a 2001 law placed limits on foreign investment. PDVSA played a key role in the December 2002 – February 2003 national strike. As a result of the strike, around 40% of the company's workforce, around 18,000 workers, were dismissed.[^cite_note-345]

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Venezuela)



[Caracas Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caracas_Metro) in Los Jardines Station

[Venezuela's airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Venezuela) include the [Simón Bolívar International Airport](8277069.md) in [Maiquetía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maiquet%C3%ADa) and [La Chinita International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Chinita_International_Airport) near [Maracaibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracaibo). Major seaports are at La Guaira, Maracaibo and [Puerto Cabello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Cabello). In the south and east, the Amazon rainforest region has limited cross-border transport. In the west, there is a mountainous border of over 2,213 kilometres (1,375 mi) shared with Colombia. The Orinoco River is navigable by oceangoing vessels up to 400 kilometres (250 mi) inland and connects the major industrial city of Ciudad Guayana to the Atlantic Ocean.

Venezuela [has a limited national railway system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_de_Ferrocarriles_del_Estado), which has no active rail connections to other countries. The Chávez administration tried to invest in expanding it, but the rail project was put on hold due to debt.[^cite_note-346] Several major cities have metro systems. The Caracas Metro has been operating since 1983. The [Maracaibo Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracaibo_Metro) and [Valencia Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valencia_Metro_\(Venezuela\)) were opened more recently. Venezuela has a road network of nearly 100,000 kilometres (62,000 mi), placing it [around 45th in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_road_network_size).[^cite_note-347] Around a third of roads are paved.

### Utilities

See also: [Electricity sector in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_Venezuela)



Electricity generation in Venezuela in terawatt-hours

The **electricity sector in Venezuela** is heavily dependent on [hydroelectricity](381399.md), with this energy source accounting for 64% of the country's [electricity generation](9540.md) in 2021.[^cite_note-\:0-95] The country relies on six hydroelectric plants, with Central Hidroeléctrica Guri providing the majority of this capacity. In 2021, [natural gas](22131.md) and [petroleum](23195.md) contributed 25% and 11% to electricity generation, respectively. Managed by [CORPOELEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corpoelec), the sector has declined due to outdated infrastructure and insufficient investment, reducing generation from 120 billion kWh in 2013 to 95 billion kWh in 2021. Despite a 99% electrification rate in 2019, frequent power outages have worsened, peaking with a nationwide blackout in 2019 and increasing by 22% from 2021 to 2022. Fuel shortages have also hampered power plant operations.[^cite_note-\:02-348]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Venezuela)

Venezuela is among the most urbanized countries in Latin America.[^cite_note-encartaSA-22][^cite_note-UNpopstats-23] About 93% of the population lives in urban areas in northern Venezuela, especially in Caracas, which is the largest city. 73% of people live less than 100 kilometres (62 mi) from the coastline.[^cite_note-349] Though almost half the land area lies south of the Orinoco, only 5% of Venezuelans live there. The largest and most important city south of the Orinoco is [Ciudad Guayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciudad_Guayana), which is the sixth most populous [conurbation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conurbation).[^cite_note-350] Other major cities include [Barquisimeto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barquisimeto), [Valencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valencia,_Carabobo), [Maracay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracay), [Maracaibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracaibo), [Barcelona-Puerto La Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelona-Puerto_La_Cruz), [Mérida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9rida,_M%C3%A9rida) and [San Cristóbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Crist%C3%B3bal,_T%C3%A1chira).

According to the 2022 revision of the [World Population Prospects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Population_Prospects)[^cite_note-UN_WPP-351][^cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-352] the total population was 28,199,867 in 2021, representing a significant increase from 5,482,000 in 1950. A 2014 study by sociologists of the [Central University of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_University_of_Venezuela) found over 1.5 million Venezuelans, or about 4% to 6% of the country's population, had left Venezuela since 1999.[^cite_note-ENHaug28-353][^cite_note-Elimpulso23AUG-354] By late 2025, the UNHCR reported approximately 7.9 million [Venezuelan refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_refugees) and migrants living worldwide, with the vast majority in Latin America.[^cite_note-unhcr-crisis-41]

### Ethnicity

Racial groups in Venezuela (2011 census):

[^cite_note-Census-ethnics-356]

1. [Moreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moreno_Venezuelans) (51.6%)

2. [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Venezuelans) (43.6%)

3. [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Venezuelans) (3.60%)

4. Others (1.20%)



A map showing the proportion of the Venezuelan population with Spanish nationality or people who are residing in Venezuela which are declared as Spanish or people with declared Iberian Spanish ancestry

The people of Venezuela come from a variety of ancestries. It is estimated that the majority of the population is of [pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo), or mixed, ethnic ancestry. In the 2011 census, which [Venezuelans](19835614.md) were asked to identify themselves according to their customs and ancestry, the term *pardo* was excluded from the answers. The majority claimed to be [moreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moreno_Venezuelans) or [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Venezuelans)—51.6% and 43.6%, respectively.[^cite_note-Census-ethnics-356] Slightly more than half of the population claimed to be *[moreno](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/moreno)*, a term used throughout Ibero-America that in this case means "dark-skinned" or "brown-skinned", as opposed to having a lighter skin. Ethnic minorities in Venezuela consist of groups that descend mainly from African or Indigenous peoples. 2.8% identified themselves as "black" and 0.7% as *afrodescendiente* (Afro-descendant), 2.6% claimed to belong to Indigenous peoples, and 1.2% answered "other races".[^cite_note-Census-ethnics-356] Among Indigenous people, 58% were [Wayúu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Way%C3%BAu), 7% [Warao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warao_people), 5% [Kariña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kari%C3%B1a), 4% [Pemón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pem%C3%B3n), 3% [Piaroa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piaroa), 3% [Jivi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guahibo_people), 3% [Añú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C3%B1%C3%BA_people?action=edit\&redlink=1), 3% [Cumanágoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuman%C3%A1goto), 2% [Yukpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yukpa), 2% [Chaima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaima) and 1% [Yanomami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanomami); the remaining 9% consisted of other Indigenous nations.[^cite_note-357] According to an autosomal DNA study conducted in 2008 by the [University of Brasília](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bras%C3%ADlia), the composition of Venezuela's population is 60.60% European, 23% Indigenous, and 16.30% African.[^cite_note-358]

During the colonial period and until after the Second World War, many of the European immigrants to Venezuela came from the [Canary Islands](5717.md)[^cite_note-359] and [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaniards_in_Venezuela)[^cite_note-360] with a relevant amount of [Galicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicians) and [Asturians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asturians). These immigrants from Spain had a significant cultural impact on the cuisine and customs of Venezuela.[^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363] These influences on Venezuela have led to the nation being called the 8th island of the Canaries.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] With the start of oil exploitation in the early 20th century, companies from the United States began establishing operations in Venezuela, bringing with them U.S. citizens. Later, during and after the war, new waves of immigrants from other parts of Europe, the Middle East, and China began; many were encouraged by government-established [immigration programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Venezuela) and lenient immigration policies.[^cite_note-Romero2010-366] During the 20th century, Venezuela, along with the rest of Latin America, received millions of immigrants from Europe.[^cite_note-PadillaPeixoto2007-367] This was especially true post-World War II, as a consequence of war-ridden Europe.[^cite_note-PadillaPeixoto2007-367][^cite_note-Brooke1992-368] During the 1970s, while experiencing an oil-export boom, Venezuela received millions of immigrants from Ecuador, Colombia, and the Dominican Republic.[^cite_note-Brooke1992-368] Due to the belief that this immigration influx depressed wages, some Venezuelans opposed European immigration.[^cite_note-Brooke1992-368] The Venezuelan government, however, were actively recruiting immigrants from Eastern Europe to fill a need for engineers. Millions of Colombians, as well as Middle Eastern and Haitian populations would continue immigrating to Venezuela into the early 21st century.[^cite_note-Romero2010-366]

According to the *World Refugee Survey 2008*, published by the U.S. Committee for Refugees and Immigrants, Venezuela hosted a population of [refugee](45547.md) and asylum seekers from Colombia numbering 252,200 in 2007. 10,600 new asylum seekers entered Venezuela in 2007.[^cite_note-World_Refugee_Survey_2008-369] In 2011, between 500,000 and one million [illegal immigrants](7124412.md) were estimated to be living in the country.[^cite_note-370]

In 2012, Venezuela's Indigenous population was estimated at 500 thousand people (2.8% of the total), distributed among 40 Indigenous peoples.[^cite_note-371] There are three [uncontacted tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncontacted_tribes) living in Venezuela. The constitution recognizes the multi-ethnic, pluri-cultural, and multilingual character of Venezuela and includes a chapter devoted to Indigenous peoples' rights, which opened up spaces for their political inclusion at national and local level in 1999. Most Indigenous peoples are concentrated in eight states along the borders with Brazil, Guyana, and Colombia. Major groups are the [Wayuu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayuu) in the west, the [Warao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warao_people) in the east, the [Yanomami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanomami) installed in the south, and the [Pemon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pemon) in the southeast.



[Moreno (Mestizo)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizos_in_Venezuela) population of Venezuela in 2011 (1 dot = 25 people, shading = population share, hexagons = higher concentration areas).



[White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Venezuelan) population of Venezuela in 2011 (1 dot = 25 people, shading = population share, hexagons = higher concentration areas).

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Venezuela)



Spanish-language sign in [Lara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lara_\(state\)), Venezuela

[Castilian Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castilian_Spanish) (*castellano*) is the official language of Venezuela and is spoken natively by 97.3% of the population.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] Although most residents are monolingual Spanish speakers, many other languages are spoken in Venezuela. In addition to Spanish, the constitution recognizes more than thirty Indigenous languages, including [Wayuu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayuu_language), [Warao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warao_language), [Pemón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pemon_language), and many others for the official use of the [Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Venezuela), mostly with few speakers – less than 1% of the total population. Wayuu is the most spoken Indigenous language, with 170,000 speakers.[^cite_note-Ethnologue-374]

Immigrants, in addition to Spanish, speak their own languages. Chinese (400,000), Portuguese (254,000),[^cite_note-Ethnologue-374] and Italian (200,000)[^cite_note-375] are the most-spoken languages after Spanish. Arabic is spoken by Lebanese and Syrian colonies on [Isla de Margarita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isla_de_Margarita), Maracaibo, [Punto Fijo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punto_Fijo), [Puerto la Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_la_Cruz), [El Tigre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Tigre), [Maracay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracay), and Caracas. Portuguese is spoken by the Portuguese community in [Santa Elena de Uairén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Elena_de_Uair%C3%A9n) and by much of the population, due to its proximity to Brazil.[^cite_note-376] The German community speaks their native language, while the people of [Colonia Tovar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonia_Tovar) speak mostly an [Alemannic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alemannic_German) dialect of German called [*alemán coloniero*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonia_Tovar_dialect).

English is the most widely used foreign language in demand and is spoken by many professionals, academics, and members of the upper and middle classes as a result of the oil exploration by foreign companies, and its acceptance as a [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca). Culturally, English is common in southern towns like [El Callao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Callao_Municipality), and the native English-speaking (English-creole speaking) influence is evident in folk and calypso songs from the region.[^cite_note-auto2-377]

Dialects of Eastern-Caribbean English-based creoles were brought to Venezuela by [Trinidadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinidadian) and other [British West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_West_Indies) immigrants.[^cite_note-auto2-377] They are collectively referred to as [Venezuelan English Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_English_Creole). A variety of [Antillean Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antillean_Creole) is spoken by a small community in El Callao and [Paria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paria_Peninsula).[^cite_note-378] Italian language teaching is delivered by private Venezuelan schools and institutions. Other languages spoken by large communities in the country are [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_language) and [Galician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician_language), among others.

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Venezuela)

Religion in Venezuela (2011):

[^cite_note-grumilla-379]

1. [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic) (71.0%)

2. [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) (17.0%)

3. [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) (7.00%)

4. Other religion (3.00%)

5. No answer (1.00%)

In 2011, 88% of the population was Christian, primarily [Roman Catholic](606848.md) (71%). The remaining 17% were [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant), primarily [Evangelicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicals). In Latin America, Protestants are usually called *"evangélicos"*. 8% of Venezuelans are irreligious. Almost 3% of the population follow another religion. 1% of the population practice [Santería](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santer%C3%ADa).[^cite_note-grumilla-379]

There are small but influential Muslim, [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze),[^cite_note-Khalifa_2013_loc=6-7-380][^cite_note-381] Buddhist, and Jewish communities. The Muslim community of more than 100,000 is concentrated among persons of Lebanese and Syrian descent living in Nueva Esparta state, Punto Fijo and the Caracas area. Venezuela is home to the largest Druze communities outside the Middle East, estimated at 60,000,[^cite_note-Los_Angeles_Times-382] and concentrated among persons of Lebanese and Syrian descent.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-Khalifa_2013_loc=6-7-380] Buddhism is practiced by over 52,000 people. The Buddhist community is made up mainly of Chinese, Japanese, and Korean people.

The Jewish community has shrunk in recent years from 22,000 in 1999[^cite_note-TOERaid-384] to less than 7,000 in 2015,[^cite_note-AJjan2015-385] due to rising economic pressures and allegedly, according to the US-based [Anti-Defamation League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Defamation_League), to [antisemitism in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_Venezuela).[^cite_note-Hurricane-386][^cite_note-SRI-387][^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] In 2010, there were 580 Hindus in Venezuela according to the [Association of Religion Data Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives).[^cite_note-390]

### Health

Main article: [Health in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Venezuela)

See also: [Healthcare in Venezuela](3877626.md) and [Mission Barrio Adentro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_Barrio_Adentro)



University Hospital, [Central University of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_University_of_Venezuela)

Venezuela has a national [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) system. The current government has created a program to expand access to health care known as [Misión Barrio Adentro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_Barrio_Adentro),[^cite_note-Venezuela_Information_Office-391][^cite_note-392] although its efficiency and work conditions have been criticized.[^cite_note-MBAeu-393][^cite_note-394][^cite_note-ABCnov2014-395] As of December 2014 an estimated 80% of Barrio Adentro establishments are abandoned.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-LPdec2014-397]

[Infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) was 19 deaths per 1,000 births for 2014, which was lower than the South American average. To compare: The U.S. figure was 6 deaths per 1,000 births in 2013.[^cite_note-ciawfb-70] In 2006, child [malnutrition](258979.md) was 17%. [Delta Amacuro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_Amacuro) and [Amazonas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazonas_\(Venezuelan_state\)) had the nation's highest rates.[^cite_note-398] In 2018, 32% of Venezuelans lacked adequate sanitation, primarily those living in rural areas.[^cite_note-399] In 2006, diseases ranging from [diphtheria](58937.md), [plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plague_\(disease\)), [malaria](20423.md),[^cite_note-Finnegan-272] [typhoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoid) fever, [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever), [cholera](7591.md), [hepatitis A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatitis_A), [hepatitis B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatitis_B), and [hepatitis D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatitis_D) were present in the country.[^cite_note-guardian1-400] In 2021, [obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity) was prevalent in approximately 30% of the adult population.[^cite_note-ciawfb-70]

In 2004, Venezuela had 150 [sewage treatment plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Venezuela). 13% of the population lacked access to drinking water, but this number had been dropping.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChávez2004-401] During the economic crisis under Maduro's presidency, medical professionals were forced to perform outdated treatments on patients, and electricity blackouts also affect care.[^cite_note-402]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Venezuela)



Venezuela's illiteracy rate, based on data from UNESCO

[^cite_note-unesco1988-403]

[^cite_note-unesco-uis-404]

&#x20;and the Instituto Nacional de Estadística (INE) of Venezuela

[^cite_note-ortega-405]

In 2008, 95.2% of the adult population was literate.[^cite_note-hdrstats.undp.org-406] The net primary school enrollment rate was at 91% and the net secondary school enrollment rate was at 63% in 2005.[^cite_note-hdrstats.undp.org-406] Universities include the [Central University of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_University_of_Venezuela) founded in 1721, the [University of Zulia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Zulia), the [University of the Andes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Andes_\(Venezuela\)), [Simón Bolívar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar_University_\(Venezuela\)), and the [University of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidad_de_Oriente).

Many graduates seek a future abroad because of the country's troubled economy and heavy crime rate. Over 1.35 million college graduates have left the country since the beginning of the Bolivarian Revolution.[^cite_note-ENHaug28-353][^cite_note-Elimpulso23AUG-354] It is believed that nearly 12% of Venezuelans live abroad, with Ireland becoming a popular destination for students.[^cite_note-407] According to Claudio Bifano, president of the Venezuelan Academy of Physical, Mathematical, and Natural Sciences, more than half of all medical graduates had left Venezuela in 2013.[^cite_note-408]

By 2018, 58% of students had dropped out of school nationwide. Areas near bordering countries had more than 80% of their students leave.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-\:2-410] Nationwide, about 93% of schools do not meet the minimum requirements to operate and 77% do not have utilities such as food, water or electricity.[^cite_note-\:2-410]

## Culture



[Música Llanera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joropo) is the most popular folk genre, and the cowboy music, of Venezuela. Depiction of a Llanera band in 1912.

The [culture of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Venezuela) is a melting pot made up of three main groups: the Indigenous Venezuelans, the Spanish, and the Africans. The Spanish influence predominates due to the colonization process and the socioeconomic structure it created. Spanish influences can be seen in the country's architecture, music, religion, and language. The Africans brought in many musical influences, especially introduction of the drum.

### Architecture



The historic center of [Coro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coro,_Venezuela), a UNESCO [World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites_by_country),

[^cite_note-411]

&#x20;is an example of [Spanish colonial architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonial_architecture) in Venezuela.



[Basilica of Our Lady of Chiquinquirá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Our_Lady_of_Chiquinquir%C3%A1,_Maracaibo), built between 1686 and completed in 1858, where is kept the colonial image of the [Virgin of Chiquinquirá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_of_Chiquinquir%C3%A1), in [Maracaibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracaibo)

[Carlos Raúl Villanueva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Ra%C3%BAl_Villanueva)[^cite_note-412] was the most important architect of the modern era; he designed the Central University of Venezuela, (a [World Heritage Site](44940.md)) and its Aula Magna. Other notable architectural works include the Capitolio, the [Baralt Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baralt_Theatre), the [Teresa Carreño Cultural Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresa_Carre%C3%B1o_Cultural_Complex), and the [General Rafael Urdaneta Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Rafael_Urdaneta_Bridge).

Prehistoric man began to build useful architecture from approximately 1000 BC to the 15th century AD, in the period known as the "Neo-Indian". Neo-Indian architecture consisted of incipient constructions, such as [agricultural terraces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_terraces) and vaults lined by stones, called mintoyes, which were used as tombs and silos for the storage of agricultural products. The Indo-Hispanic architecture developed from 1498 onward.[^cite_note-413] Colonial architecture is built from the 16th century, when Venezuela began to be a dependent colony of the Spanish Empire,[^cite_note-414] until 1810, when the process of independence began. The architecture of this period is characterized by its discreet modesty, with the exception of some cities. The renunciation of most of the decorative elements and variegated ostentations of fanciful baroque, the impossibility of using expensive materials and the consequent lack of craftsmen, contributed to establish a modest but well-defined physiognomy of the colonial architecture.

Christian temples from the colonial era were constituted by an almost invariable arrangement consisting of a rectangular plan, three naves separated by arches of [alfarje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfarje) roofing composed of religious architecture in colonial times. The society dedicated a great amount of resources to erect religious monuments comparable to those of other countries of the continent. The 17th century was of reconstruction of the [Catholic churches](606848.md) that had been destroyed by the earthquake of 1641. In the 18th century, specifically between 1728 and 1785, the prosperity that Venezuela enjoyed due to the opening of the [Compañía Guipuzcoana de Caracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compa%C3%B1%C3%ADa_Guipuzcoana_de_Caracas)[^cite_note-415] was also reflected in the construction of new architecture, especially of a religious nature.

### Art



[Antonio Herrera Toro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Herrera_Toro), self portrait 1880

[Venezuelan art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_art) was initially dominated by religious motifs. However, in the late 19th century, artists began emphasizing historical and heroic representations of the country's struggle for independence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENg200431-416][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAponte200845-417] This move was led by [Martín Tovar y Tovar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mart%C3%ADn_Tovar_y_Tovar).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAponte200845-417][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETarverFrederick200610-418] [Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism) took over in the 20th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETarverFrederick200610-418] Notable [Venezuelan artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_artists) include [Arturo Michelena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Michelena), [Cristóbal Rojas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crist%C3%B3bal_Rojas_\(artist\)), [Armando Reverón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armando_Rever%C3%B3n), [Manuel Cabré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Cabr%C3%A9); the [kinetic artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinetic_art) [Jesús Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jes%C3%BAs_Soto), [Gego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gego) and [Carlos Cruz-Diez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Cruz-Diez);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETarverFrederick200610-418] and contemporary artists such as [Marisol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marisol_Escobar) and [Yucef Merhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yucef_Merhi).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFichner-Ratus2012519-419][^cite_note-420]

### Literature

[Venezuelan literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_literature) originated soon after the Spanish conquest of the mostly pre-literate Indigenous societies.[^cite_note-421] It was originally dominated by Spanish influences. Following the rise of political literature during the war of independence, Venezuelan [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism), notably expounded by Juan Vicente González, emerged as the first important genre in the region. Although mainly focused on narrative writing, Venezuelan literature was advanced by poets such as [Andrés Eloy Blanco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Eloy_Blanco) and [Fermín Toro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferm%C3%ADn_Toro).

Major writers and novelists include [Rómulo Gallegos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%B3mulo_Gallegos), [Teresa de la Parra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresa_de_la_Parra), [Arturo Uslar Pietri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Uslar_Pietri), [Adriano González León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adriano_Gonz%C3%A1lez_Le%C3%B3n), [Miguel Otero Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Otero_Silva), and [Mariano Picón Salas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Pic%C3%B3n_Salas). The great poet and humanist [Andrés Bello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Bello) was also an educator and intellectual (He was also a childhood tutor and mentor of [Simón Bolívar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar)). Others, such as [Laureano Vallenilla Lanz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laureano_Vallenilla_Lanz) and [José Gil Fortoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Gil_Fortoul), contributed to Venezuelan [Positivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positivism).

### Music

Main article: [Music of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Venezuela)



The Guanaguanare dance, a popular dance in [Portuguesa State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguesa_\(state\))

The Indigenous musical styles are exemplified by groups like Un Solo Pueblo and [Serenata Guayanesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serenata_Guayanesa). The national musical instrument is the [cuatro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuatro_\(Venezuela\)). Traditional musical styles and songs mainly emerged in and around the llanos region, including "Alma llanera" (by [Pedro Elías Gutiérrez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_El%C3%ADas_Guti%C3%A9rrez) and [Rafael Bolívar Coronado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Bol%C3%ADvar_Coronado)), "Florentino y el diablo" (by [Alberto Arvelo Torrealba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Arvelo_Torrealba)), "Concierto en la llanura" by [Juan Vicente Torrealba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Vicente_Torrealba), and ["Caballo viejo"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caballo_Viejo) (by [Simón Díaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_D%C3%ADaz)).

[Zulian gaita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaita_zuliana) is popular, generally performed during Christmas. The national dance is the [joropo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joropo).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECortés20132134-422] Venezuela has always been a melting pot of cultures and this can be seen in the richness and variety of its musical styles and dances: [calipso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calypso_music), [bambuco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bambuco), [fulía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ful%C3%ADa), cantos de pilado de maíz, cantos de lavanderas, sebucán, and maremare.[^cite_note-423] [Teresa Carreño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresa_Carre%C3%B1o) was a world-famous 19th-century piano virtuoso. Recently, great classical music performances have come out of Venezuela. The [Simón Bolívar Youth Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar_Youth_Orchestra) has hosted a number of excellent concerts in many European concert halls, most notably at the 2007 London [Proms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proms), and has received several honors. The orchestra is the pinnacle of [El Sistema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Sistema), a publicly financed, voluntary music education program now being emulated in other countries.

In the early 21st century, a movement known as "Movida Acústica Urbana" featured musicians trying to save some national traditions, creating their own original songs but using traditional instruments.[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-425] Some groups following this movement are Tambor Urbano,[^cite_note-426] Los Sinverguenzas, C4Trío, and Orozco Jam.[^cite_note-427]

Afro-Venezuelan musical traditions are most intimately related to the festivals of the "black folk saints" San Juan and [St. Benedict the Moor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedict_the_Moor). Specific songs are related to the different stages of their festivals and processions, when the saints start their yearly "*paseo*" – stroll – through the community.

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Venezuela)

#### Baseball

See also: [Baseball in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_in_Venezuela)



[Monumental Stadium of Caracas Simón Bolívar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumental_Stadium_of_Caracas_Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar) is used by the [Venezuelan Professional Baseball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_Professional_Baseball_League) and has a capacity of approximately 40,000 spectators.

The origins of baseball in Venezuela are unclear, although it is known that the sport was being played in the country by the late 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENicholsMorse2010306-428] In the early 20th century, North American immigrants who came to Venezuela to work in the nation's oil industry helped to popularize the sport.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWardrope200337-429] During the 1930s, baseball's popularity continued to rise, leading to the foundation of the [Venezuelan Professional Baseball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_Professional_Baseball_League) in 1945, and the sport became the nation's most popular.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJozsa201312-430][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGibson200618-431]

Venezuela competed in the [2026 World Baseball Classic](73255749.md) for the tenth time. On 14 March 2026, after securing a vital victory against [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_national_baseball_team) in the quarterfinals, Venezuela qualified for the historically first time in the six-team baseball tournament for [2032](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2032_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-432] They then defeated [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italy_national_baseball_team) to qualify for the inaugural final. Venezuela beat the [United States](4119270.md) in the championship game by a final score of 3–2.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]

The popularity of baseball in the country makes Venezuela a rarity among its South American neighbors—association football is the dominant sport in the continent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWardrope200337-429][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGibson200618-431][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENicholsMorse2010307-435] However, being one of the Latin American baseball powerhouses, Venezuela remarkably has not competed in the [Olympics tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_at_the_Summer_Olympics) which began from [1992 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Summer_Olympics) in [Barcelona](4443.md), unlike more famous teams such as [Dominican Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Republic_national_baseball_team) and [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rico_national_baseball_team).[^cite_note-436]

#### Football

See also: [Football in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Venezuela)

Although not as popular in Venezuela as the rest of South America, football, spearheaded by the [Venezuela national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuela_national_football_team) is gaining popularity as well. The sport is also noted for having an increased focus during the World Cup.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAalgaard200454-437] Venezuela is scheduled to host the [Copa América](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copa_Am%C3%A9rica) every 40 years.[^cite_note-438]

#### Others

Basketball is behind baseball and football are among the more popular sports.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAalgaard200454-437] Venezuela hosted the [2012 Basketball World Olympic Qualifying Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_FIBA_World_Olympic_Qualifying_Tournament_for_Men) and the [2013 FIBA Basketball Americas Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Americas_Championship), which took place in the [Poliedro de Caracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poliedro_de_Caracas).

Venezuela is home to former [Formula 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_1) driver, [Pastor Maldonado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastor_Maldonado).[^cite_note-Strickland2015-439] Maldonado has increased the reception of Formula 1 in Venezuela, helping to popularize the sport in the country.[^cite_note-440]

[Rubén Limardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rub%C3%A9n_Limardo) won a gold medal in [fencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fencing_at_the_2012_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-441] In the Winter Sports, [Cesar Baena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cesar_Baena) had represented the country since 2008 in Nordic Skiing, the first South American skier to compete in a FIS Cross Country Ski World Cup on Düsseldorf 2009.

Economic growth and redistribution policies led to a significant decline in poverty, from 50 percent in 1998 to approximately 30 percent in 2013, according to official figures. Inequality also decreased, as reflected in the decrease in the Gini Index, from 0.49 in 1998 to 0.40 in 2012, among the lowest rates in the region. Nevertheless, the collapse in international oil prices, along with inadequate macro and microeconomic policies, have significantly affected Venezuela's economic and social performance. The country's reliance on the hydrocarbon sector has sharply increased (oil now accounts for 96 percent of exports).

[^cite_note-WSJElectionDatabase-157]: &#x20;Vyas, Kejal; Dubé, Ryan (30 July 2024). Retrieved 30 July 2024. The Venezuelan opposition has moved to show concrete evidence that the election was stolen. ... The opposition said it had collected data from 73% of the country's voting stations on its own and compiled it in a publicly accessible database on Tuesday. Using their national identification, Venezuelans can sign in and review a scanned tally sheet from their voting station showing how many votes went to each candidate. ... 'I found mine, the proof is there,' said Celina Ramirez, an opposition supporter who said she was able to log into the website to locate the tally sheet from her east Caracas voting center, which she said showed González receiving the lion's share of votes. 'There's no way the regime can fool everyone with their tricks,' she added ... The Carter Center, one of the few international organizations invited to monitor the elections, has urged Venezuela's government to release comprehensive polling data at the local level, which is needed to assess the electoral process. ...The opposition hopes the release of the database will increase pressure on Maduro's autocratic regime to make public the detailed results of the election. Also available from [MSN](https://www.msn.com/en-us/news/world/venezuela-s-maduro-moves-to-silence-opposition-after-disputed-election/ar-BB1qTSWi) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20240823220053/https://www.msn.com/en-us/news/world/venezuela-s-maduro-moves-to-silence-opposition-after-disputed-election/ar-BB1qTSWi) 23 August 2024 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine).

[^cite_note-357]: &#x20;Benítez, Deivis (2011) \[Updated 13 August 2012]. ["Poblaciones Indígenas en aumento según censo poblacional 2011"](https://web.archive.org/web/20130116234901/http://www.minpi.gob.ve/minpi/es/noticias/1548-np1428) (in Spanish). PRENSA MINPPPI. Archived from [the original](http://www.minpi.gob.ve/minpi/es/noticias/1548-np1428) on 16 January 2013. Retrieved 10 October 2012. Los resultados arrojados por el censo poblacional realizado por el Instituto Nacional de Estadísticas en el 2011 demuestra que las poblaciones indígenas ha aumentado progresivamente con respecto al censo del año 2001.\
    &#x20;\
    &#x20;Según los datos estadísticos publicados por el INE, el total de población que se declaró indígena por sexo, arrojó un resultado de 50,46% hombre y 49,54% mujeres representando 365.920 hombres y 359.208 mujeres para un total de 725.148 personas que se declararon indígenas de Venezuela.\
    &#x20;\
    &#x20;Así mismo, se tomó el porcentaje de población por entidad donde el estado Zulia es la entidad con más indígenas con un 61%, seguido del estado Amazonas con 10%, Bolívar con un 8%, Delta Amacuro con 6%, Anzoátegui 5%, Sucre 3%, Apure y Monagas 2% mientras que en otras entidades existe un 3% de población indígena.\
    &#x20;\
    &#x20;Entre tanto, los pueblos indígenas con mayor población se encuentran los Wayuu 58%, Warao 7%, Kariña 5%, Pemón 4%, Piaroa, Jivi, Añu, Cumanágoto 3%, Yukpa, Chaima 2%, el pueblo Yanomami 1% y otros pueblos con un 9%.
